Proposition 2

(Size explosion in MELL)
.

\rho_{n}\rightarrow_{l_{0}}^{3(n-1)}\pi_{n}
,
|\rho_{n}|={\mathcal{O}}(n)
, and
|\pi_{n}|=\Omega(2^{n})
.

Proof 3.2
.

By induction on
n
. The bounds on the sizes follow immediately from the definition and the i.h. For
n=1
, we have
\rho_{1}=\pi_{1}
, so the statement holds. For
n+1
, we have that by i.h.
\rho_{n}\rightarrow_{l_{0}}^{3(n-1)}\pi_{n}
. Therefore, we obtain:

\rho_{n+1}

=

\pi_{1}
!
\rho_{n}
!
\mathtt{cut}

\rightarrow_{l_{0}}^{3(n-1)}

\pi_{1}
!
\pi_{n}
!
\mathtt{cut}

=

\mathtt{ax}
\mathtt{ax}
\otimes
{\mathsf{d}}
{\mathsf{d}}
\mathtt{c}
!
\tau_{n}
...
{\mathsf{c}}
!
\mathtt{cut}

\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{c}}0}

\mathtt{ax}
\mathtt{ax}
\otimes
{\mathsf{d}}
{\mathsf{d}}
!
\mathtt{cut}
\tau_{n}
...
!
\tau_{n}
...
!
\mathtt{cut}
...
\mathtt{c}
\mathtt{c}
...
{\mathsf{c}}
...

\rightarrow_{\boxbox 0}\rightarrow_{\boxbox 0}

\mathtt{ax}
\mathtt{ax}
\otimes
{\mathsf{d}}
{\mathsf{d}}
\mathtt{cut}
\tau_{n}
...
!
\tau_{n}
...
!
\mathtt{cut}
!
\mathtt{c}
\mathtt{c}
...
{\mathsf{c}}
...

=

\tau_{n+1}
...
{\mathsf{c}}
!

=

\pi_{n+1}
!
.

The size exploding family of Prop.
2
is a chain of what Roversi and Vercelli in
[
DBLP:conf/fopara/RoversiV09
]
call
spindles
, which they identify as the problematic configurations for representing polynomial time.

Typing the Size Exploding Family

The proof net
\rho_{1}=\pi_{1}
used to build the family can be typed by assigning type
A
, for an arbitrary type
A
, to the right conclusion of the axioms (thus having
A^{\bot}
on the left conclusion of the axioms), and
A_{1}:=!(A\otimes A)
for the right conclusions of
\rho_{1}
(and
?A^{\bot}
on the left conclusion of
\rho_{1}
). To type the second term of the family
\rho_{2}
, and assuming that the left occurrence of
\pi_{1}
in
\rho_{2}
is typed as just described, the right one needs to be typed using
A_{1}
as type of the axioms, thus obtaining as type of the right conclusion,
A_{2}:=!(A_{1}\otimes A_{1})=!(!(A\otimes A)\otimes!(A\otimes A))
. It is then easily seen that the type
A_{n+1}
of the right conclusion of
\rho_{n+1}
is given by
A_{n+1}=!(A_{n}\otimes A_{n})
, that is, the type size grows exponentially with
n
.

Size Explosion and Elementary Linear Logic

The proofs
\rho_{n}
of the size exploding family actually belong to
elementary linear logic
(ELL): they use derelictions—which are forbidden in ELL—but those derelictions can be seen as the auxiliary ports of functorial promotions associated to the
!
s, which are allowed in ELL. Therefore, size explosion affects ELL as well. We mention this fact because ELL has some interesting properties with respect to Lévy’s
optimal reduction
. On the one hand, ELL provides a much simpler implementation of optimal reduction, because the technical
oracle
part of the implementation is not needed in ELL, as pointed out by Asperti
[
DBLP:conf/lics/Asperti98
]
. On the other hand, the complex behaviour of duplication responsible for the
unreasonability
of optimal reduction is already fully present in ELL, and it is thus independent of the oracle, as shown by Asperti et al.
[
DBLP:conf/popl/AspertiCM00
]
. For the study of time cost models, restricting to E(ME)LL does not seem to simplify the problem, as size explosion affects both MELL and EMELL with respect to reduction at level 0. This is rather in line with the results of
[
DBLP:conf/popl/AspertiCM00
]
.

4.
The Linear Substitution Calculus

Here we recall the basics of the linear substitution calculus, which is the footprint of the exponential substitution calculus that shall be introduced in the next section.

There are various ingredients. First of all, the syntax of the
\lambda
-calculus is extended with explicit substitutions
t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]
(shortened to ESs), which is a constructor binding
x
in
t
(meant to be substituted by
s
)—and a compact notation for
{\sf let}\ x=s\ {\sf in}\ t
—while we use
t\{x{\shortleftarrow}s\}
for meta-level substitution.

\begin{array}[]{r\colspace\colspace rlll}\textsc{LSC Terms}&t,s,u&::=&x\mid\lambda x.t\mid ts\mid t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\end{array}

The LSC is based on the
rejection
of commuting rules for ESs. In traditional
\lambda
-calculi with ESs, a term such as
(ts)[x{\shortleftarrow}u]
would rewrite to
t[x{\shortleftarrow}u]s[x{\shortleftarrow}u]
by commuting the ES and the application. In the LSC it does not: ESs interact directly with variable occurrences, using
contexts
to specify a commutation-free rewriting system.
Contexts, used pervasively in the study of the LSC, are terms with a
hole

\langle\cdot\rangle
intuitively standing for a removed sub-term. Here, we need (general) contexts
C
and the special case of substitution contexts
L
(standing for
L
ist of substitutions).

\begin{array}[]{r\colspace\colspace rlll}\textsc{Contexts}&C&::=&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid Ct\mid tC\mid\lambda x.{C}\mid C[x{\shortleftarrow}t]\mid t[x{\shortleftarrow}C]\\
\textsc{Substitution contexts}&L&::=&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid L[x{\shortleftarrow}t]\end{array}

Replacing the hole of a context
C
with a term
t
(or another context
C^{\prime}
) is called
plugging
and noted
C\langle t\rangle
(resp.
C\langle C^{\prime}\rangle
).
In general, plugging does capture free variables, that is,
(\lambda x.\langle\cdot\rangle x)\langle x\rangle=\lambda x.xx
. We use
C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}t\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
for a plugging with on-the-fly renaming of bound variables of
C
to avoid capture in
t
. For instance,
(\lambda x.\langle\cdot\rangle x)\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\lambda y.xy
.

The LSC has three rewriting rules. As usual, the root rules are extended to be applied everywhere in a term via a context closure. An unusual aspect is that two of the rules also use contexts in the definition of the root rules.

\begin{array}[]{rrll}\lx@intercol\hfil\textsc{LSC Root rewriting rules}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\textsc{Beta at a distance}&L\langle\lambda x.t\rangle s&\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}}&L\langle t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\rangle\\
\textsc{Linear Substitution}&C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}[x{\shortleftarrow}s]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{ls}}&C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}s\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\\
\textsc{Garbage Collection}&t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}}&t\ \ \ \mbox{if }x\notin\mathtt{fv}(t)\par\end{array}

Contextual closure
:

t\mapsto_{a}t^{\prime}

C\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{a}C\langle t^{\prime}\rangle

(
a\in\{\mathtt{dB},\mathtt{ls},\mathtt{gc}\}
)

For
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}
, the notation means that given
t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]
with
x\in\mathtt{fv}(t)
we consider a decomposition
t=C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
isolating a free occurrence of
x
(one among possibly many). The rule is inspired by replication in the
\pi
-calculus. Examples of steps:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(\lambda x.xy)[y{\shortleftarrow}t]su&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}&(xy)[x{\shortleftarrow}s][y{\shortleftarrow}t]u\\
\lambda x.((\lambda y.xx)[z{\shortleftarrow}t])&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}}&\lambda x.\lambda y.xx\\
(\lambda y.xx)[x{\shortleftarrow}t][z{\shortleftarrow}s]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}&(\lambda y.xt)[x{\shortleftarrow}t][z{\shortleftarrow}s]\\
(\lambda y.xx)[x{\shortleftarrow}t][z{\shortleftarrow}s]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}&(\lambda y.tx)[x{\shortleftarrow}t][z{\shortleftarrow}s]\end{array}

The LSC is a very well behaved rewriting system (see
[
DBLP:conf/rta/Accattoli12
,
DBLP:conf/popl/AccattoliBKL14
,
DBLP:conf/rta/BarenbaumB17
]
), conservatively extending the
\lambda
-calculus, and improving it in various respects.

Micro-Step Simulates Small-Step

Let
|t|_{x}
the number of free occurrences of
x
in
t
. The LSC simulates meta-level substitution as follows:

\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}^{|t|_{x}}&t\{x{\shortleftarrow}s\}[x{\shortleftarrow}s]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}}&t\{x{\shortleftarrow}s\}\end{array}

that is, by first replacing one by one the occurrences of
x
using
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}
, and then collecting
[x{\shortleftarrow}s]
when none are left. Therefore, the LSC simulates
\beta
reduction, as
(\lambda x.t)s\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}^{*}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}}t\{x{\shortleftarrow}s\}
.

Strong Normalization

We define strong normalization as it is relevant for the next property, and shall be used also later on. Given a reduction relation
\rightarrow
, the predicate
t
is
SN_{\rightarrow}
, also noted
t\in SN_{\rightarrow}
, is defined
inductively
as follows:

•

t
is
SN_{\rightarrow}
if
t
is
\rightarrow
-normal, i.e., has no
\rightarrow
redexes.

•

t
is
SN_{\rightarrow}
if
s
is
SN_{\rightarrow}
for all
s
such that
t\rightarrow s
.

We use
SN_{\rightarrow}
for the set of terms that are
SN_{\rightarrow}
, and say that
\rightarrow
is strongly normalizing (or
SN
) if
t\in SN_{\rightarrow}
for all terms
t
.

Local Termination

While terms in the LSC can be divergent, because the LSC simulates the
\lambda
-calculus, an important property is that the rewriting rules of the LSC are strongly normalizing when considered separately.

Proposition 3

(Local termination,
[
DBLP:conf/rta/Accattoli12
]
)
.

Let
\rightarrow\in\{\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}},\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}},\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}}\}
. Then
\rightarrow
is strongly normalizing.

The only non-trivial point is the strong normalization of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}
, which requires a measure. It easily extends to the one of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}}
, that is, of the sub-system rewriting ESs. The ESC shall preserve this key property, as we shall prove in Section
8
.

Structural Equivalence

The LSC is often enriched with the following equivalence.

{defi}

[Structural equivalence]
Define head contexts as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r\colspace\colspace rlll}\textsc{Head contexts}&H&::=&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid Ht\mid\lambda x.{H}\mid H[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\end{array}

If
x\notin\mathtt{fv}(H)
and
H
does not capture variables in
\mathtt{fv}(s)
, set:

\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}H\langle t\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}s]&\sim&H\langle t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\rangle\end{array}

Structural equivalence

\equiv
is the closure of
\sim
by reflexivity, symmetry, transitivity, and contexts
C
.

Its key property is that it commutes with evaluation in the following strong sense.

Proposition 4

(
\equiv
is a strong bisimulation wrt
\rightarrow_{LSC}

[
DBLP:conf/popl/AccattoliBKL14
]
)
.

Let
a\in\{\mathtt{dB},\mathtt{ls},\mathtt{gc}\}
. If
t\equiv s\rightarrow_{a}u
then exists
r
such that
t\rightarrow_{a}r\equiv u
.

Essentially,
\equiv
never creates redexes, it can be postponed, and vanishes on normal forms (that have no ESs). Accattoli shows that
\equiv
is exactly the quotient induced by the (call-by-name) translation of
\lambda
-terms with ESs to proof nets
[
DBLP:conf/ictac/Accattoli18
]
.

Linear Head Reduction

One of the key properties of the LSC is that it admits a neat definition of
linear head reduction
, a notion first studied by Danos and Regnier
[
Danos04headlinear
]
and Mascari and Pedicini
[
DBLP:journals/tcs/MascariP94
]
. Before the introduction of the LSC, the presentations of linear head reduction (in
[
Danos04headlinear
,
DBLP:journals/tcs/MascariP94
]
) were very technical and hard to manage. To define it in the LSC, we need the head contexts of Definition
4
. They are used twice. Firstly, head contexts are used to define the root linear head substitution rule:

\begin{array}[]{r\colspace\colspace rllll}\textsc{Linear head substitution}&H\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}[x{\shortleftarrow}s]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{lhs}}&H\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}s\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\end{array}

Secondly, the root rules
\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}}
,
\mapsto_{\mathtt{lhs}}
, and
\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}}
are closed by
head
contexts:

Contextual closures

t\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}

H\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lh}\mathtt{dB}}H\langle t^{\prime}\rangle

t\mapsto_{\mathtt{lhs}}t^{\prime}

H\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lhs}}H\langle t^{\prime}\rangle

t\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}}t^{\prime}

H\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lh}\mathtt{gc}}H\langle t^{\prime}\rangle

\begin{array}[]{c\colspace ccc}\textsc{Linear head reduction}&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lh}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lh}\mathtt{dB}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lhs}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lh}\mathtt{gc}}\end{array}

Linear head reduction has many interesting property. For the present paper, the most relevant one is the
sub-term property
, which, for the given presentation, is proved by Accattoli and Dal Lago in
[
DBLP:conf/rta/AccattoliL12
]
(it was already used by Danos and Regnier and Mascari and Pedicini, but not exploited for complexity analyses).

Theorem 5

(Sub-term property for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lh}}
,
[
DBLP:conf/rta/AccattoliL12
]
)
.

All the sub-terms duplicated by
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lhs}}
along a linear head evaluation sequence
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lh}}^{*}s
are sub-terms of
t
(up to variables renaming).

The good strategy for IMELL that we shall define in Section
generalizes linear head reduction to the whole of IMELL and preserves its sub-term property. For that, note that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lh}}
never evaluates inside ESs. Similarly, the good strategy shall not evaluate inside cuts. Note also that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lh}}
does not compute normal forms, as it never evaluates arguments. Since we want the good strategy to compute cut-free proofs, we shall have to evaluate the analogous of arguments in our framework (namely, the left premises of subtractions). This shall be obtained by entering into arguments only under some conditions, similarly to how
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{lh}}
is generalized to enter inside arguments by Accattoli and co-authors
[
DBLP:journals/corr/AccattoliL16
,
DBLP:conf/aplas/AccattoliBM15
]
, obtaining
linear leftmost-outermost reduction
.

5.
Towards Exponentials as Substitutions

This section is an informal and hopefully intuitive introduction to our cut elimination for IMELL. The logical rules we refer to are standard (but decorated with terms), see Fig.
4
.

Splitting Terms

For the moment, we prefer to avoid giving grammars, but we want nonetheless to fix some terminologies and intuitions. We distinguish variables into multiplicatives, noted
m,n,o
, and exponentials, noted
e,f,g
, and use
x,y,z
for variables of unspecified kind. Proof terms for axioms and right introduction rules shall be called
values
, and noted
v
. We borrow from the LSC the notation
L
(here standing for
L
eft context) and generalize it to a context corresponding to (the term annotations induced by) a sequence of left rules of the sequent calculus. Exactly as IMELL proofs can be seen as ending on a sequence of left rules following a right rule or an axiom, every proof term
t
shall uniquely (on-the-fly) decompose as
L\langle v\rangle
, called its
splitting
.

Meta-Level Substitution

For the design of our system, the first step is understanding the subtle interplay between linearity and substitution. Consider a cut on an exponential formula, and a first attempt at decorating it with terms:

\Gamma\vdash s\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A

\Delta,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash t\,{:}\,B

\mathtt{cut}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}s{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\,{:}\,B

Note that the notation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}s{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
for cuts is similar but opposite to the one for ESs in the LSC, as the arrow goes from left to right and it binds on the right (in
t
), to better reflect the structure of sequent proofs. We seek a notion of meta-level substitution
\{s{\shortrightarrow}e\}t
and a small-step rule such as:

\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}s{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t&\rightarrow&\{s{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\end{array}

(3)

Perhaps surprisingly, linear logic does not validate this rule. Despite the
{\mathsf{!}}A
type, not all of
s
, indeed, might be allowed to be duplicated. Let’s refine the example and consider the case in which the last rule contributing to
s
is, say, the left rule for
\otimes
, what we shall call a
par
and annotate with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}
:

\Gamma,x\,{:}\,B,y\,{:}\,C\vdash s^{\prime}\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A

\otimes_{l}

\Gamma,m\,{:}\,B\otimes C\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s^{\prime}\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A

\Delta,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash t\,{:}\,B

\mathtt{cut}

\Gamma,m\,{:}\,B\otimes C,\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\,{:}\,B

Now we can see why (
3
) is not valid: the annotation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}
being linear, it should not be duplicated. The proof nets approach to this issue is marking with a
box
the sub-term/proof net of
s^{\prime}
that can be duplicated. The usual sequent calculus approach, instead, is commuting the cut with
\otimes_{l}
, which on proof terms corresponds to have a rule such as:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t&\rightarrow&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}s^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\end{array}

and then keep commuting the cut with left rules until an introduction of a
{\mathsf{!}}
connective on the right—what we call
an exponential value
, noted
v_{\mathtt{e}}
—is reached, which can then trigger the substitution. But these commutative rules are a burden, and exactly what the LSC philosophy rejects.

The idea is to exploit the splitting of terms into values and left contexts, so as to import on terms the proof nets approach. A term
s
of exponential type, in fact, shall always be splittable as
L\langle v_{\mathtt{e}}\rangle
, that is, as a left context and an exponential value. We then refine (
3
) as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}s{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}L\langle v_{\mathtt{e}}\rangle{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t&\rightarrow&L\langle\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\rangle\end{array}

Meta-level substitution thus concerns only exponential values
v_{\mathtt{e}}
—playing the role of proof nets boxes—and
not
the surrounding left context
L
. This same mechanism is used in Accattoli and Paolini’s
value substitution calculus

[
DBLP:conf/flops/AccattoliP12
]
.

Split Cuts

In fact, we adopt a further refinement. We restrict cuts to have only values as left sub-terms, that is, of being of shape
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
. Since we want to stick to the standard sequent calculus for IMELL, we do not restrict the typing rule, we do instead modify its decoration:

\Gamma\vdash s=L\langle v\rangle\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A

\Delta,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash t\,{:}\,B

\mathtt{cut}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\,{:}\,B

And we shall do the same for the left rule
\multimap_{l}
for
\multimap
(see Fig.
4
). This refinement simplifies various technical points, in particular it enables a simple definition of the good strategy.

Micro-Step Substitution

For micro-step substitution we simply borrow the LSC approach, adopting rules such as:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}&\rightarrow&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{e}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\end{array}

While in line with the LSC, this is
not
how cut elimination is usually performed in both proof nets and the sequent calculus. There are three main differences:

(1)

LSC-style duplications
: in our case duplication happens also when there are no contractions involved, for instance we shall have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}e\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}v_{\mathtt{e}}
, and there shall be a rule (
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
) duplicating and interacting with derelictions at the same time (see the next section). Not only this approach is perfectly sound, it actually has better rewriting properties than the traditional one.

(2)

No commutations
: there are no commutations with
{\mathsf{!}}
. Even in proof nets, where most commutative cuts vanish, micro-step cut elimination usually relies on a commutative rule for
{\mathsf{!}}
-boxes (namely
\rightarrow_{\boxbox}
in Fig.
1
), that on terms looks as follows (when
e\in\mathtt{fv}(t)
):

\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\mathsf{!}}t&\rightarrow&{\mathsf{!}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\end{array}

The LSC philosophy rejects such a commutative rule.

(3)

Axioms
: in the proof nets literature, cut elimination of exponential axioms is identical to the one for multiplicative axioms (there is in fact no distinction between the two kinds of axioms) and simply amounts to the removal of the cut and the axiom, in both the small-step (
à la
Regnier) and the micro-step (
à la
Girard) approaches. In (asymmetric) intuitionistic settings, axioms have two rewriting rules, depending on whether they are substituted, or substituted upon. In our syntax, the traditional approach to cut elimination for exponential axioms would take the following form:

\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t&\rightarrow&\{f{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\end{array}

When written with terms, one immediately notices that such a rule is small-step. At the end of Section
10
, we shall show that such a small-step rule generates a closable but unpleasant local confluence diagram with the other micro-step exponential rules, which is avoided by adopting a LSC-duplication style also for exponential axioms/variables.

6.
The Exponential Substitution Calculus

\begin{array}[]{rrllll}\textsc{Multiplicative vars}&m,n,o&\in&\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{m}}\\
\textsc{Exponential vars}&e,f,g&\in&\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{e}}\\
\textsc{Variables}&x,y,z&\in&\mathcal{V}:=\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{m}}\uplus\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{e}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rrllll}\textsc{Multiplicative values}&v_{\mathtt{m}}&::=&m\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{m}}\mid(t,s)\mid\lambda x.t\\
\textsc{Exponential values}&v_{\mathtt{e}}&::=&e\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{e}}\mid{\mathsf{!}}t\\
\textsc{Values}&v&::=&v_{\mathtt{m}}\mid v_{\mathtt{e}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rrllll}\textsc{Terms}&t,s,u&::=&v\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rrllll}\textsc{Left ctxs}&L&::=&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}L\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}L\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}L\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}L\\
\textsc{Value ctxs}&V&::=&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid(C,s)\mid(t,C)\mid\lambda x.C\mid{\mathsf{!}}C\\
\textsc{General ctxs}&C&::=&V\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}V{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}V,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\mid L\langle C\rangle\\
\textsc{Mult. value ctxs}&V_{\mathtt{m}}&::=&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid(M,s)\mid(t,M)\mid\lambda x.M\\
\textsc{Mul. ctxs}&M&::=&V_{\mathtt{m}}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}V_{\mathtt{m}}{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}V_{\mathtt{m}},x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\mid L\langle M\rangle\end{array}

Figure 3.
Grammars of the exponential substitution calculus.

Values and Terms

The grammars of the ESC are in Fig.
3
. First of all, a disclaimer. The constructors of the calculus are an untyped
minimalist encoding
of the sequent calculus rules, and
not
an intuitively readable calculus. For that aim, one would rather use
\mathtt{let\mbox{-}in}
notations. Variables are of two disjoint kinds, multiplicative and exponential, and we often refer to variables of unspecified kind using the notations
x,y,z
. Values are the proof terms associated to axioms or to the right rules and, beyond variables, are
abstractions

\lambda x.t
,
tensor pairs

(t,s)
, and
promotions

{\mathsf{!}}t
. The proof terms decorating left rules are
pars

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t
,
subtractions

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t
,
derelictions

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t
, and
cuts

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
, which is in red because of its special role. Note that cuts and subtractions are
split
, that is, have values (rather than terms) as left sub-terms. Back to readability, subtraction might also be decorated with
{\sf let}\ x=mv\ {\sf in}\ t
, but note that this notation is less faithful to the logical rule because the two premises are represented asymmetrically, and that it uses an application constructor that rather belongs to natural deduction.

In
\lambda x.t
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t
, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
we have that
x
is bound in
t
, and in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t
both
x
and
y
are bound in
t
. We identify terms up to
\alpha
-renaming. Free variables (resp. multiplicative/exponential variables) are defined as expected, and noted
\mathtt{fv}(t)
(resp.
\mathtt{mfv}(t)
and
\mathtt{efv}(t)
). We use
|t|_{x}
for the number of free occurrences of
x
in
t
.
There is a notion of
proper term
ensuring the linearity of multiplicative variables. The only relevant case is:
{\mathsf{!}}t
is proper if
t
is proper and
\mathtt{mfv}(t)=\emptyset
.

{defi}

[Proper terms]

Proper terms are defined by induction on
t
as follows:

•

Variables
:
m
and
e
are proper.

•

Tensor
:
(t,s)
is proper if
t
is proper,
s
is proper, and
\mathtt{mfv}(t)\cap\mathtt{mfv}(s)=\emptyset

•

Par
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t
is proper if
t
is proper,
m\notin(\mathtt{mfv}(t)\setminus{x,y})
,
x\neq y
, and if
x
(resp.
y
) is a multiplicative variable then
x\in\mathtt{mfv}(t)
(resp.
y\in\mathtt{mfv}(t)
).

•

Implication
:
\lambda x.t
is proper if
t
is proper and if
x
is a multiplicative variable then
x\in\mathtt{mfv}(t)
.

•

Subtraction
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t
is proper if
t
is proper,
v
is proper,
\mathtt{mfv}(v)\cap(\mathtt{mfv}(t)\setminus\{x\})=\emptyset
,
m\notin\mathtt{mfv}(t)
, and if
x
is a multiplicative variable then
x\in\mathtt{mfv}(t)
.

•

Bang
:
{\mathsf{!}}t
is proper if
t
is proper and
\mathtt{mfv}(t)=\emptyset
.

•

Dereliction
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t
is proper if
t
is proper and if
x
is a multiplicative variable then
x\in\mathtt{mfv}(t)
.

•

Cut
:
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
is proper if
t
is proper,
v
is proper,
\mathtt{mfv}(v)\cap(\mathtt{mfv}(t)\setminus\{x\})=\emptyset
, and if
x
is a multiplicative variable then
x\in\mathtt{mfv}(t)
.

\begin{array}[]{rclll}\textsc{Formulas}&A,B,C&::=&X_{\mathtt{m}}\mid A\otimes B\mid A\multimap B\mid{\mathsf{!}}A\end{array}

Multiplicative right rules

A\neq{\mathsf{!}}B

\mathtt{ax}_{\mathtt{m}}

m\,{:}\,A\vdash m\,{:}\,A

\Gamma\vdash t\,{:}\,A

\Delta\vdash s\,{:}\,B

\Gamma\#\Delta

\otimes_{r}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash(t,s)\,{:}\,A\otimes B

x\,{:}\,A,\Gamma\vdash t\,{:}\,B

\multimap_{r}

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t\,{:}\,A\multimap B

Multiplicative left rules

\Gamma\vdash L\langle v\rangle\,{:}\,A

\Delta,x\,{:}\,A\vdash t\,{:}\,B

\Gamma\#(\Delta,x\,{:}\,A)

\mathtt{cut}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\,{:}\,B

\Gamma,x\,{:}\,A,y\,{:}\,B\vdash t\,{:}\,C

m
fresh

\otimes_{l}

\Gamma,m\,{:}\,A\otimes B\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\,{:}\,C

\Gamma\vdash L\langle v\rangle\,{:}\,A

\Delta,x\,{:}\,B\vdash t\,{:}\,C

\Gamma\#(\Delta,x\,{:}\,B)
,
m
fresh

\multimap_{l}

\Gamma,\Delta,m\,{:}\,A\multimap B\vdash L\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\,{:}\,C

Exponential rules

\mathtt{ax}_{\mathtt{e}}

e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A

\Gamma,x\,{:}\,A\vdash t\,{:}\,B

e
fresh

{\mathsf{!}}_{l}

\Gamma,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\,{:}\,B

{\mathsf{!}}\Gamma\vdash t\,{:}\,A

{\mathsf{!}}_{r}

{\mathsf{!}}\Gamma\vdash{\mathsf{!}}t\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A

\Gamma\vdash t\,{:}\,A

e
fresh

{\mathsf{w}}

\Gamma,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}B\vdash t\,{:}\,A

\Gamma,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}B,f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}B\vdash t\,{:}\,A

{\mathsf{c}}

\Gamma,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}B\vdash\{e{\shortrightarrow}f\}t\,{:}\,A

Figure 4.
IMELL typing rules for the ESC, where
\Gamma\#\Delta
is a shortcut for
\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)\cap\mathtt{dom}(\Delta)=\emptyset
.

Typing System

The typing rules are in Fig.
4
. The formulas of IMELL and the deductive rules of the sequent calculus are standard, but for the decoration with proof terms and the side conditions about variable names of the form
\Gamma\#\Delta
, which is a shortcut for
\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)\cap\mathtt{dom}(\Delta)=\emptyset
. Linear implication
\multimap
is also referred to as
lolli
. The only atomic formula that we consider,
X_{\mathtt{m}}
, is multiplicative. There is no multiplicative unit because in presence of the exponentials
1
can be simulated by
!(X_{\mathtt{m}}\multimap X_{\mathtt{m}})
. We distinguish between multiplicative and exponential axioms, in order to decorate them with the corresponding kind of variable.

Note that the weakening and contraction rules do not add constructors to terms. This is crucial in order to keep the calculus manageable. Note also that the decorations of the cut and
\multimap_{l}
rules are
split
, as explained in the previous section. Clearly, typed terms are proper. We use
\pi\;\triangleright\Gamma\vdash t\,{:}\,A
for a proof/typing derivation
\pi
ending in that sequent.

Contexts and Plugging

The broadest notion of context that we consider is
general contexts

C
, which simply allow the hole
\langle\cdot\rangle
to replace any sub-term in a term. Because of split cuts and subtractions, the definition relies on the auxiliary notion of
value context

V
. The definition also uses
left contexts

L
, which are contexts under left constructors (or, for binary left constructors, under the right sub-term) that play a key role in the system—their use in defining
C
is just to keep the grammar compact (to fit it in the figure).

A fact used pervasively is that every term
t
writes, or
splits
uniquely as
t=L\langle v\rangle
. For instance
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}(m,n)
splits as
L={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}\langle\cdot\rangle
and
v=(m,n)
. We also have
multiplicative contexts

M
, that the proof nets literature would call
level 0
contexts, that is, contexts with the hole out of all
{\mathsf{!}}
, and which also rely on their own value contexts
V_{\mathtt{m}}
.

Because of split cuts and subtractions, plugging is slightly tricky, as it has to preserve the split shape. For instance, we have
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\langle\cdot\rangle{\shortrightarrow}o{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t)\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}(m,n)\rangle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}(m,n){\shortrightarrow}o{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
.
A similar approach to plugging is also used by Accattoli et al. in
[
DBLP:conf/ppdp/AccattoliCGC19
]
. The full definition follows. It is mostly as expected, the only two subtle cases are for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\langle\cdot\rangle{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}\langle\cdot\rangle,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}
.

{defi}

[Plugging]

Plugging
C\langle t\rangle
is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rll@{\hspace{.4cm}}rlll}\lx@intercol\hfil\textsc{Plugging}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\langle\cdot\rangle\langle t\rangle&:=&t\hfil\hskip 11.38092pt&(\lambda x.C)\langle t\rangle&:=&\lambda x.C\langle t\rangle\\
(C,s)\langle t\rangle&:=&(C\langle t\rangle,s)\hfil\hskip 11.38092pt&(s,C)\langle t\rangle&:=&(s,C\langle t\rangle)\\
({\mathsf{!}}C)\langle t\rangle&:=&{\mathsf{!}}C\langle t\rangle\hfil\hskip 11.38092pt&({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}C)\langle t\rangle&:=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}C\langle t\rangle\\
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}C)\langle t\rangle&:=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}C\langle t\rangle\hfil\hskip 11.38092pt&({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}C)\langle t\rangle&:=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}C\langle t\rangle\\
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C)\langle t\rangle&:=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle t\rangle\hfil\hskip 11.38092pt\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rlll|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}V,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s)\langle t\rangle&:=&\begin{cases}L\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s\rangle&\mbox{if }V=\langle\cdot\rangle\mbox{ and }t=L\langle v\rangle\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}V\langle t\rangle,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}V{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}s)\langle t\rangle&:=&\begin{cases}L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}s\rangle&\mbox{if }V=\langle\cdot\rangle\mbox{ and }t=L\langle v\rangle\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}V\langle t\rangle{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}s&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{array}

Plugging
C\langle C^{\prime}\rangle
of contexts is defined similarly, and plugging is extended to all forms of contexts by seeing them as general contexts. As for the LSC, plugging can capture variables and we use
C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}t\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
when we want to prevent it.

With our notion of plugging, a left constructor in a term might be identified via plugging of a term in a context in various ways, e.g. for
t:=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}m{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}n,f)
we have
t=C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}m\rangle
with
C:=({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\langle\cdot\rangle{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}n,f)
, and
t=C^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}m{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}n\rangle
with
C^{\prime}:=(\langle\cdot\rangle,f)
. For most uses, in particular for defining the rewriting rules, this is harmless. It shall play a role, however, for defining the good strategy, because it shall be important whether the hole of the context is inside or outside a cut. To this purpose, note that in the first decomposition the term
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}m
is not a sub-term of
t
. This suggest the following notion.

{defi}
[Positions]

A
position
in a term
t
is a decomposition
t=C\langle s\rangle
such that
s
is a sub-term of
t
(as for
C^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}m{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}n\rangle
above).

We can now state the property ensured by properness.

{toappendix}

Lemma 6

(Structural linearity)
.

Let
t
be a proper term and
m\in\mathtt{mfv}(t)
. Then
|t|_{m}=1
and
t=M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
for some
M
.

Proof 6.1
.

By induction on
t
.

\begin{array}[]{rlll}\lx@intercol\hfil\textsc{Root multiplicative rules}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[9.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{m}}{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}&\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}&M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{m}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}n{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t&\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}&\{n{\shortrightarrow}m\}t\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}(s,u){\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle&\mapsto_{\otimes}&M\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}L^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\rangle\par\\
&&\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{with }s=L\langle v\rangle\mbox{ and }u=L^{\prime}\langle v^{\prime}\rangle\\[3.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\lambda y.s{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle&\mapsto_{\multimap}&M\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\\
&&\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{with }s=L\langle v^{\prime}\rangle\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rlll}\lx@intercol\hfil\textsc{Micro-step root exponential rules}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[9.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}&\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{e}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle&\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}s{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle&\mapsto_{{\mathsf{!}}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}s{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\\
&&\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{with }s=L\langle v\rangle\\[3.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t&\mapsto_{\mathtt{w}}&t\ \ \ \ \mbox{if }e\notin\mathtt{fv}(t)\end{array}

Small-step root exponential rule

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t&\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}&\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\end{array}

Contextual closure

t\mapsto_{a}s

C\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{a}C\langle s\rangle

for
a\in\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2},\otimes,\multimap,\\
&\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2},{\mathsf{!}},\mathtt{w},\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}\end{aligned}\right\}

\begin{array}[]{rlllc}\lx@intercol\hfil\textsc{Notations}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\textsc{Multiplicative}&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}\cup\rightarrow_{\otimes}\cup\rightarrow_{\multimap}\\
\textsc{Exponential micro-step}&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}\\
\textsc{Small-step}&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ss}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}\\
\textsc{Micro-step}&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}\\
\textsc{Non-erasing micro-step}&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}\\
\textsc{Non-lolli micro-step}&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\multimap}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}\cup\rightarrow_{\otimes}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}\\
\textsc{Non-lolli small-step}&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ss}_{\neg\multimap}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}\cup\rightarrow_{\otimes}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}\end{array}

Figure 5.
Rewriting rules of the ESC.

Multiplicative Cut Elimination Rules

The rewriting rules are in Fig.
5
. The ESC has four multiplicative rules, in particular two for axioms, depending on whether they are acted upon (
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}
) or used to rename another multiplicative (thus linear) variable (
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}
). Rule
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}
is expressed generically for multiplicative values
v_{\mathtt{m}}
(that is, multiplicative variables
m
, abstractions
\lambda m.t
, and tensor pairs
(t,s)
). For
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}
, we abuse notations and use a form of meta-level substitution
\{n{\shortrightarrow}m\}t
for the renaming operation. There is also a slight superposition between
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}
, for instance in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}m{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}\lambda e.n\rightarrow\lambda e.m
, which can be both kinds of steps. To disambiguate, one should refine
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}
into two rules:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}n{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}^{\prime}}&M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}n{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\\[3.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}n{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}^{\prime\prime}}&M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}n{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\end{array}

But the ambiguity is harmless and the notation is convenient. Note that in
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}
,
\rightarrow_{\otimes}
, and
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
(and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}^{\prime}/\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}^{\prime\prime}}
), the cut acts on a sub-term inside a multiplicative context
M
, that is, out of
!
. Moreover, it is silently assumed that
M
does not capture
m
in
\rightarrow_{\otimes}
and
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
(and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}^{\prime}/\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}^{\prime\prime}}
), but it might capture other variables in
t
and
v
.

In both
\rightarrow_{\otimes}
and
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
the rule has to respect split cuts, which is why, for writing the reduct, the sub-terms
s
and
u
get split on-the-fly. We suggest to have a look at the proof of subject reduction on page
14
, which shows how some of the rewriting steps act on the decorated proof.
An example of
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
step follows:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\lambda e.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}m{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}n{\varogreaterthan}{\mathsf{!}}f,o{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}o&\rightarrow_{\multimap}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}m{\shortrightarrow}o{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}o.\end{array}

Micro-Step Exponential Rules

There are also four micro-step exponential rules, with again two rules for axioms. Replacement of variables (
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}
) and erasure (
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
) are expressed generically for exponential values
v_{\mathtt{e}}
(that is, exponential variables
f
and promotions
{\mathsf{!}}t
), interaction with derelictions (in
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
) instead requires inspecting
v_{\mathtt{e}}
.

Rule
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
removes the dereliction, copies the promotion body, and puts it in a cut—in proof nets jargon,
it opens the box
. To preserve the split shape, the body of the promotion is split and only the value is cut. An example:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}g{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}g{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}\lambda m.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}e^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}(e^{\prime},m)&\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}g{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}g{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}\lambda m.{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}g{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}g{\shortrightarrow}e^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}(e^{\prime},m).\end{array}

Note that
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
entangles
interaction with a dereliction
and
duplication
, which is not what proof nets usually do, as mentioned in Section
5
. It is silently assumed that
C
does not capture
e
in
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
but it might capture other variables in
t
.

Meta-Level Substitution and the Small-Step Exponential Rule

For defining the small-step exponential cut elimination we need meta-level substitution
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t
, which is defined only for exponential values. The long definition is mostly as expected, but for the case of derelictions, explained after the definition.

{defi}
[Meta-level exponential substitution]

The meta-level (exponential) substitution
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t
of the exponential value
v_{\mathtt{e}}
for the free occurrences of
e
in
t
is defined by induction on
t
as follows (assuming on-the-fly
\alpha
-renaming to avoid capture of free variables, omitted for ease of reading and manipulation):

Meta-level substitution

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{.2cm}}l@{\hspace{.2cm}}ll}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}m\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&m\\
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}f\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&f\\
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}e\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&v_{\mathtt{e}}\\
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}(t,u)\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&(\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t,\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}u)\\
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}\lambda x.t\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&\lambda x.\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\\
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}{\mathsf{!}}t\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&{\mathsf{!}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{.2cm}}l@{\hspace{.2cm}}ll}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\\
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\\
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\\
\{f{\shortrightarrow}e\}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}\{f{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\\
\{{\mathsf{!}}L\langle v\rangle{\shortrightarrow}e\}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}\{{\mathsf{!}}L\langle v\rangle{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\rangle\\
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\hskip 5.69046pt&:=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\end{array}

The key cases are the substitution of a value for a dereliction (the second and third to last in the right column), where the definition depends on the shape of the value. For promotions, the definition mimics the micro-step rule
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
, making a copy of the content of the box and splitting it on-the-fly. An example:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}\{{\mathsf{!}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}g{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}g{\shortrightarrow}e\}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}e^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}(e^{\prime},e)&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}g{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}g{\shortrightarrow}e^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}(e^{\prime},{\mathsf{!}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}g{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}g).\end{array}

Our definition verifies the expected properties of meta-level substitution. Some are given by the next two lemmas.

Lemma 7

(Basic properties of meta-level substitution)
.

(1)

If
v_{\mathtt{e}}
and
t
are proper then
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t
is proper.

(2)

\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}v_{\mathtt{e}}^{\prime}
is an exponential value and
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}v_{\mathtt{m}}
is a multiplicatie value.

(3)

If
C
does not capture free variables of
v_{\mathtt{e}}
then
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}C\langle t\rangle=(\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}C)\langle\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\rangle
.

(4)

\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}s=s
if
e\notin\mathtt{fv}(s)
.

(5)

\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f\}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t=\{\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f\}v_{\mathtt{e}}^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}e\}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f\}t
.

(6)

\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}f\}u=\{v_{\mathtt{e}}^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}f\}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}u
if
e\notin\mathtt{fv}(s)
and
e\notin\mathtt{fv}(u)
.

(7)

\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}\{n{\shortrightarrow}m\}u=\{n{\shortrightarrow}m\}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}u
.

(8)

\{{\mathsf{!}}u{\shortrightarrow}e\}\{{\mathsf{!}}u{\shortrightarrow}f\}t=\{{\mathsf{!}}u{\shortrightarrow}e\}\{e{\shortrightarrow}f\}t
.

Lemma 8

(Stability of steps under substitution)
.

(1)

If
t\rightarrow_{a}s
then
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\rightarrow_{a}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}s
for
a\in\{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2},\otimes,\multimap,\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}},\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}\}
.

(2)

If
v_{\mathtt{e}}\rightarrow_{a}v_{\mathtt{e}}^{\prime}
then
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\rightarrow_{a}^{*}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t
for
a\in\{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2},\otimes,\multimap,\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2},{\mathsf{!}},\mathtt{w}\}
.

Proof 6.2
.

(1)

By induction on
t\rightarrow_{a}s
. Details are in
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-lics
]
. The only subtle case is the one for
\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}
, which has a sub-case where the step might become a
\mapsto_{{\mathsf{!}}}
step after subtitution. Let
t={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}g{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u\rangle\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}g{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}g{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u\rangle=s
,
g=e
, and
v_{\mathtt{e}}={\mathsf{!}}L\langle v\rangle
. Then:

\begin{array}[]{rllll}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}t&=&\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}g{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u\rangle\\
&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u\rangle\\
&=_{{L.\ref{l:bang-subs-prop}.\ref{p:bang-subs-prop-zero}}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}(\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}C)\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}u\rangle\\
&\mapsto_{{\mathsf{!}}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}(\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}C)\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}u\rangle\rangle\\
&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}(\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}C)\langle\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}g{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u\rangle\\
&=_{{L.\ref{l:bang-subs-prop}.\ref{p:bang-subs-prop-zero}}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}g{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u\rangle\\
&=&\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}g{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}g{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u\rangle\par&=&\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}g\}s\end{array}

(2)

By induction on
t
.

The small-step exponential rule
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}
then simply turns a cut
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
into the corresponding meta-level substitution.

Linear Implication and Cut Creations

Rule
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
is special in that it is the only rule of the ESC that
creates
cuts. Note indeed that in the root
\mapsto_{\multimap}
rule:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\lambda y.L\langle v\rangle{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v^{\prime},x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle&\mapsto_{\multimap}&M\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\end{array}

the cut
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}
in the reduct is not a cut of the reducing term. One might argue that also
\mapsto_{\otimes}
and
\mapsto_{{\mathsf{!}}}
do create cuts, because in their root rules, recalled here:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}(L\langle v\rangle,L^{\prime}\langle v^{\prime}\rangle){\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle&\mapsto_{\otimes}&M\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}L^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\rangle\\[6.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}L\langle v\rangle{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle&\mapsto_{{\mathsf{!}}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}L\langle v\rangle{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\end{array}

the cuts
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}
of the reducts are not in the reducing terms. The difference is that
v
and
v^{\prime}

do occur as sub-terms of cuts
in the reducing terms of
\mapsto_{\otimes}
and
\mapsto_{{\mathsf{!}}}
, while in
\mapsto_{\multimap}
the value
v^{\prime}
does not. We then say that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}
is
created
in
\mapsto_{\multimap}
.
This fact shall play a key role for
local termination
(Theorem
26
below) and for the good strategy. Being specific to
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
, cut creations are a feature of the intuitionistic setting. That is, they are
invisible
in classical linear logic.

Variable Occurrences and Redex Positions

For later defining the good strategy, we identify a redex with its position, which is a context, as it is done for the LSC by Accattoli and Dal Lago
[
DBLP:journals/corr/AccattoliL16
]
. Every step
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}s
reduces a redex of shape
t=C\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C^{\prime}\langle t_{x}\rangle\rangle
where
t_{x}
is an
occurrence
of
x
, i.e. a sub-term of
t
of shape
x
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}x{\invamp}y,z{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}x{\varogreaterthan}v,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s
, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}x{{\mathsf{?}}}y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s
. The
redex position of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}
steps
is the context
C\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C^{\prime}\rangle
. The
redex position of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}
steps
is the context closing the root step. We write
C:t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}/\mathtt{ss}}s
for a redex of position
C
in
t
, the reduction of which produces
s
.

We conclude the section with the fact that proper terms are stable by reduction.

Lemma 9
.

Let
C\langle t\rangle
be a proper term. Then:

(1)

t
is proper.

(2)

If
s
is proper and such that
\mathtt{mfv}(s)=\mathtt{mfv}(t)
then
C\langle s\rangle
is proper.

Proof 6.3
.

By induction on
C
.

Lemma 10
.

Let
t
be a proper term and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}s
. Then
s
is proper.

Proof 6.4
.

Let
t=C\langle u\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}C\langle r\rangle=s
. By Lemma
9
,
u
is proper, and to prove the statement it is enough to prove that if
u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}r
is a root step then
r
is proper and
\mathtt{mfv}(r)=\mathtt{mfv}(u)
. Note that all the root rules preserve the set of multiplicative variables, that is,
\mathtt{mfv}(r)=\mathtt{mfv}(u)
holds. Proving that the root rules preserve being proper is straightforward.

Preservation of properness by
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}
follows from
full composition
(Prop.
11
), just below.

7.
Basic Properties

We now prove various basic properties of the ESC. An expected property that we do not prove is the simulation of the LSC by the ESC. It can be obtained via Girard’s (call-by-name) encoding of intuitionistic logic into linear logic. It is not surprising and yet it is technical, for reasons (the encoding of applications via subtractions) that are intrinsic to the translation of natural deduction to sequent calculus and orthogonal to the features of the ESC, which is why it is omitted.

Full Composition.

First of all, micro-step exponential rules simulate the small-step one. The ESs literature calls this fact
full composition
.

Proposition 11

(Full composition, or micro-step simulates small-step)
.

If
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}s
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}^{+}s
.

Proof 7.1
.

Let

t=C\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}u\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}C\langle\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}u\rangle=s
. We treat the case
v_{\mathtt{e}}={\mathsf{!}}r
, the case
v_{\mathtt{e}}=f
is analogous. The proof is by induction on
k:=|u|_{e}
. If
k=0
then
C\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}u\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}C\langle u\rangle=C\langle\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}u\rangle
. Otherwise,
u
writes has
C^{\prime}\langle e\rangle
or
C^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u^{\prime}\rangle
. Consider the first case. Then:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}C\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}u^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C^{\prime}\langle e\rangle\rangle&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}&C\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}u^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C^{\prime}\langle{\mathsf{!}}u^{\prime}\rangle\rangle&=:&s^{\prime}\end{array}

Now note that
s^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}s
and that in
s^{\prime}
we have
|C\langle{\mathsf{!}}u^{\prime}\rangle|_{e}<k
. Then we can apply the i.h., obtaining
s^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}^{+}s
, that is,
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}^{+}s
. The case
u=C^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u^{\prime}\rangle
is analogous, simply using
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
instead of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}
.

Corollary 12
.

Let
t
be a proper term and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ss}}s
. Then
s
is proper.

Subject Reduction.

The next property is that the defined untyped cut elimination respects the typing system, that is, subject reduction holds. We first need a lemma.

Lemma 13
.

Let
\pi\;\triangleright\Gamma\vdash t\,{:}\,A
. We have
A={\mathsf{!}}B
if and only if
t=L\langle v_{\mathtt{e}}\rangle
.

Proof 7.2
.

If
A={\mathsf{!}}B
, then consider
t=L\langle v\rangle
. The proof is by induction on
L
. Clearly, if
L=\langle\cdot\rangle
then the last rule of
\pi
is necessarily an exponential axiom or a right introduction of
{\mathsf{!}}
, that is,
v=e
or
v={\mathsf{!}}s
, that is,
v
is an exponential value. If
L\neq\langle\cdot\rangle
then it follows from the i.h.

The reasoning is analogous if
A\neq{\mathsf{!}}B
.

{toappendix}

Theorem 14

(Subject reduction)
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash t\,{:}\,A
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}s
. Then
\Gamma\vdash s\,{:}\,A
.

Proof 7.3
.

By induction on
\pi\;\triangleright\Gamma\vdash t\,{:}\,A
. If
\pi
is an axiom then
t
cannot reduce. If the last rule is a cut then
t=L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}s\rangle
. If it is the root cut that it is reduced there are a number of cases given next. Otherwise (that is, if the last rule is not a cut, or if the reduced cut is not the one of the last rule) the statement follows by the i.h.

By Lemma
13
, if the cut formula is
{\mathsf{!}}B
for some
B
then
x=e
and
v=v_{\mathtt{e}}
, and otherwise
x=m
and
v=v_{\mathtt{m}}
. We show two representative cases, the
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
one, which shows how to interpret the splitting of terms into value and left contexts on proofs, and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}
, which shows how LSC-style duplication acts on proofs. The other cases are similar.

•

\mapsto_{\multimap}
.

\Gamma\vdash v\,{:}\,B

L

\Gamma^{\prime},y\,{:}\,A\vdash L\langle v\rangle\,{:}\,B

\multimap_{r}

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\lambda y.L\langle v\rangle\,{:}\,A{\multimap}B

L^{\prime}

\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\vdash L^{\prime}\langle\lambda y.L\langle v\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,A{\multimap}B

\Delta\vdash v^{\prime}\,{:}\,A

L^{\prime\prime}

\Delta^{\prime}\vdash L^{\prime\prime}\langle v^{\prime}\rangle\,{:}\,A

\Pi,x\,{:}\,B\vdash t\,{:}\,C^{\prime}

\multimap_{l}

\Delta^{\prime},\Pi,m\,{:}\,A{\multimap}B\vdash L^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v^{\prime},x,t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}\rangle\,{:}\,C^{\prime}

\Pi^{\prime},m\,{:}\,A{\multimap}B\vdash M\langle L^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v^{\prime},x,t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,C^{\prime}

\mathtt{cut}

\Gamma^{\prime\prime},\Pi^{\prime}\vdash L^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\lambda y.L\langle v\rangle{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle L^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v^{\prime},x,t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}\rangle\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,C^{\prime}

\Delta\vdash v^{\prime}\,{:}\,A

L^{\prime\prime}

\Delta^{\prime}\vdash L^{\prime\prime}\langle v^{\prime}\rangle\,{:}\,A

\mathtt{cut}

\Delta^{\prime},y\,{:}\,A,\Pi\vdash L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\,{:}\,C^{\prime}

\mathtt{cut}

M

\Gamma^{\prime},\Pi^{\prime}\vdash M\langle L^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,C^{\prime}

L^{\prime}

\Gamma^{\prime\prime},\Pi^{\prime}\vdash L^{\prime}\langle M\langle L^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,C^{\prime}

•

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}
. The step is
L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,B\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,B
. To avoid analyzing many similar (simple) sub-cases, let us assume that
L
does not capture
f
and that
f\notin\mathtt{fv}(L)
.
Now there still are two cases, depending on the multiplicity of
e
in
C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle
.

–

e\notin\mathtt{fv}(C)
. Then:

\mathtt{ax}_{\mathtt{e}}

f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A

L

\Gamma,f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash L\langle f\rangle\,{:}\,A

\Pi,x\,{:}\,A\vdash t\,{:}\,B

{\mathsf{!}}_{l}

\Pi,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\,{:}\,B

C

\Delta,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\,{:}\,B

\mathtt{cut}

\Gamma,f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A,\Delta\vdash L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,B

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}

\mathtt{ax}_{\mathtt{e}}

f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A

L

\Gamma,f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash L\langle f\rangle\,{:}\,A

\Pi,x\,{:}\,A\vdash t\,{:}\,B

{\mathsf{!}}_{l}

\Pi,g\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}g{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\,{:}\,B

C

\Delta,g\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}g{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\,{:}\,B

{\mathsf{w}}

\Delta,g\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}g{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\,{:}\,B

\mathtt{cut}

\Gamma,f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A,g\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A,\Delta\vdash L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}g{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,B

{\mathsf{c}}

\Gamma,f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A,\Delta\vdash L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,B

–

e\in\mathtt{fv}(C)
. Then in the sequence of rules corresponding to the context
C
there is a first contraction acting on the dereliction which splits the sequence of rules in two, that is, there exists
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
such that
C=C^{\prime}\langle C^{\prime\prime}\rangle
and:

\mathtt{ax}_{\mathtt{e}}

f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A

\Pi,x\,{:}\,A\vdash t\,{:}\,B

{\mathsf{!}}_{l}

C^{\prime\prime}

\Delta^{\prime},e^{\prime}\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A,g\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash C^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}g{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\,{:}\,B

{\mathsf{c}}

\Delta^{\prime},e^{\prime}\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash C^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e^{\prime}{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\,{:}\,B

C^{\prime}

\Delta,e\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A\vdash C^{\prime}\langle C^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,B

\mathtt{cut}

\Gamma,f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A,\Delta\vdash L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,B
