\Gamma,f\,{:}\,{\mathsf{!}}A,\Delta\vdash L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle\,{:}\,B

Full composition extends subject reduction to
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}
.

Clashes.

The presence of many constructors in an untyped setting gives rise to
clashes
, that is, irreducible cuts.

{defi}
[Clash, clash-free terms]
A
clash
is a term of the form
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{m}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}(t,s){\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u\rangle
, or
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\lambda x.t{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}y,z{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u\rangle
and in these cases we also say that the root cut is
clashing
. A term
t
is
clash-free
if, co-inductively,

(1)

No sub-terms of
t
are clashes, and

(2)

If
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}s
then
s
is clash-free.

Note that there are no purely exponential clashes. The second point of the next lemma uses subject reduction.

Lemma 15
.

Let
t
be a proper term.

(1)

If
t
has no clashes and
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}
then
t
is cut-free.

(2)

If
t
is typable then it is clash-free.

Proof 7.4
.

(1)

Suppose by contradiction that
t
has a cut. Then
t=C\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}s\rangle
. If
x
is exponential then, since there are no clashes,
v
is also exponential. Then there is a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}
redex, and, by full composition, a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}
redex, absurd. If
x=m
is multiplicative then so is
v
. By structural linearity (Lemma
6
),
s
has one of the following shapes:

•

M\langle m\rangle
. Then there is a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}
redex, absurd.

•

M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}y,z{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s\rangle
. Since
t
has no clashes,
v
is either a multiplicative variable, and then there is a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}
redex, or a tensor pair, and then there is a
\rightarrow_{\otimes}
redex, absurd.

•

M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}s\rangle
. Since
t
has no clashes,
v
is either a multiplicative variable, and then there is a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}
redex, or an abstraction, and then there is a
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
redex, absurd.

(2)

By co-induction on the definition of being clash-free. It is easily seen that clashes are not typable. So if
t
is typable then it has no clashes. Now, if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}s
then by subject reduction (Theorem
14
)
s
is typable. By the co-inductive hypothesis,
s
is clash-free. Therefore,
t
is clash-free.

Postponement of Garbage Collection

The micro-step rule
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
, that models garbage collection, can be postponed. The same happens in the LSC,
but
not
in the
\lambda
-calculus: for instance, the first step in the sequence
(\lambda x.\lambda y.y)su\rightarrow_{\beta}(\lambda y.y)u\rightarrow_{\beta}u
is erasing but cannot be postponed.

Lemma 16

(Local postponement of garbage collection)
.

If
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}s
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}^{+}s
.

Proof 7.5
.

By induction on
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}u
and case analysis of
u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}s
. Cases:

•

Root
:
t={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}s
. Then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}s\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}s
.

•

Inductive cases different from cut
: we treat the case of tensor, the others are similar. Let
t=(r,p)\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}(r^{\prime},p)=u
. If the
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}
step takes place in
r^{\prime}
then we apply the i.h., otherwise
(r^{\prime},p)\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}(r^{\prime},p^{\prime})=s
and we simply have
(r,p)\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}(r,p^{\prime})\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}(r^{\prime},p^{\prime})
.

•

Inductive case left of cut
:
t={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}p\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}p=s
. If the
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}
step takes place in
v^{\prime}
we use the i.h., and if it is in
p
then the two steps are disjoint and swap. The last possibility is that the root cut itself is the active one in the
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}
redex. Now, the key observation is that swapping the step
s\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}u
before the
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
one amounts to add a cut (the erased one) to both
u
and
s
, and to remove it with the postponed
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
step, which can be done smoothly because the rewriting rules do not depend on the cuts surrounding the sub-terms of the redex nor those surrounding the redex. The only cases where something interesting happens are those for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
when the
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
step is in the duplicated exponential value. For instance, if
f\notin\mathtt{fv}(t)
we have the following diagram.
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}V\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}V\langle t\rangle{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}V\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}V\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}V\langle t\rangle{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}V\langle t\rangle\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}V\langle t\rangle{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}V\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
\mathtt{w}
\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}
\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}
\mathtt{w}
\mathtt{w}

•

Inductive case right of cut
:
t={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}p\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}p^{\prime}=s
. If the
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}
step takes place in
p^{\prime}
we use the i.h., and if it is in
v
then the two steps are disjoint and swap. The last possibility is that the root cut itself is the active one in the
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}
redex. As in the previous case the swap happens smoothly because the rewriting rules do not depend on the cuts surrounding the subterms of the redex nor those surrounding the redex. There are no interesting cases.

Proposition 17

(Postponement of garbage collection)
.

if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}^{*}s
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\mathtt{w}}}^{*}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}^{*}s
.

Proof 7.6
.

It is an instance of a well-known rewriting property: the local swap in Lemma
16
is an instance of Hindley’s strong postponement property, which implies postponement.

Cut Equivalence

Similarly to the structural equivalence of the LSC, we consider a notion of cut equivalence, which is a strong bisimulation and it is postponable.

{defi}

[Cut equivalence]
If
x\notin\mathtt{fv}(M)
and
M
does not capture variables in
\mathtt{fv}(v)
, define:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle t\rangle&\sim_{\mathtt{cut}}&M\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle.\end{array}

Cut equivalence

\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
is the closure of
\sim_{\mathtt{cut}}
by reflexivity, symmetry, transitivity, and general contexts.

To prove that cut equivalence is a strong bisimulation in the small-step case, we need the following substitutivity property.

Lemma 18

(Stability by substitution of
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
)
.

(1)

If
t\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}s
then
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}s
for all
v_{\mathtt{e}}
.

(2)

If
v_{\mathtt{e}}\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}v_{\mathtt{e}}^{\prime}
then
\{v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}\{v_{\mathtt{e}}^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}e\}t
.

Proof 7.7
.

Easy inductions on
t\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}s
and
t
. Details in
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-lics
]
.

Proposition 19
.

Let
\rightarrow\in\{\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ss}},\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}\}
.

(1)

Strong bisimulation
:
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
is a strong bisimulation with respect to
\rightarrow
, preserving also the kind of step.

(2)

Structural stability of SN
: if
t\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}s
and
t\in SN_{\rightarrow}
then
s\in SN_{\rightarrow}
.

(3)

Postponement of
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
: if
t(\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}\rightarrow\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}})^{k}s
then
t\rightarrow^{k}\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}s
.

Proof 7.8
.

(1)

It is a very long but otherwise straightforward check of all possible diagrams, spelled out in
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-lics
]
. The following two diagrams for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}
are where Lemma
18
is used.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}s
\{v{\shortrightarrow}y\}s
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}s^{\prime}
\{v{\shortrightarrow}y\}s^{\prime}
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}
\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}s
\{v{\shortrightarrow}y\}s
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}s
\{v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y\}s
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}
\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}

(2)

By induction on
t\in SN_{\rightarrow}
. One simply uses the fact that
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
is a strong bisimulation, given by the previous point, and the i.h.

(3)

By induction on
k
, using the strong bisimulation property.

Cut equivalence shall play a role in the following sections. Note that its postponement property means that it is
never needed
for cut elimination to progress. And on cut-free terms, obviously, it vanishes.

There is also a larger
left equivalence

\equiv_{\mathtt{left}}
defined as
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
but including every left constructor (cut, par, subtraction, and dereliction), which is also a strong bisimulation. As mentioned in Section
4
,
\equiv
is the quotient induced by proof nets on the LSC. Intuitively,
\equiv_{\mathtt{left}}
is the quotient induced by proof nets on the ESC. There are, however, no notions of proof nets realizing such a quotient, as weakenings require non-canonical
jumps
for IMELL, that can instead be avoided for the LSC, as shown by Accattoli
[
DBLP:conf/ictac/Accattoli18
]
.

A possibly interesting point is that we shall need cut equivalence, in particular for the proof of strong normalization, while there is no need of left equivalence for any of the properties studied in this paper.

Stability Under Renaming

The last basic property that we prove is the stability of the rules and of their strong normalization under renaming.

Lemma 20

(Renaming)
.

Let
\rightarrow\in\{\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ss}},\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}\}
and
x
and
y
be two variables of the same
multiplicative/exponential kind such that
\{x{\shortrightarrow}y\}t
is proper.

(1)

Stability of steps under renaming
:
if
C:t\rightarrow_{a}s
then
\{y{\shortrightarrow}x\}C:\{y{\shortrightarrow}x\}t\rightarrow_{a}\{y{\shortrightarrow}x\}s
for
a\in\{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2},\otimes,\multimap,\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2},{\mathsf{!}},\mathtt{w},\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}\}
.

(2)

Stability of SN under renaming
:
if
t\in SN_{\rightarrow}
then
\{x{\shortrightarrow}y\}t\in SN_{\rightarrow}
.

Proof 7.9
.

(1)

Straightforward induction on the rewriting step.

(2)

By induction on
t\in SN_{\rightarrow}
. Since renaming cannot create, erase, duplicate, or change the kind of redexes,
if
\{x{\shortrightarrow}y\}t\rightarrow s
then there exists
u
such that
t\rightarrow u
and

\{x{\shortrightarrow}y\}u=s
. Then by i.h. on
u
we obtain
\{x{\shortrightarrow}y\}u=s\in SN_{\rightarrow}
.

8.
Local termination

Here we are going to prove that the ESC has the same local termination property of the LSC, thus showing that one can claim that it realizes the slogan
exponentials as substitutions
.

Divergence

Let us first show that evaluation in the untyped ESC can diverge. We adapt the usual looping combinator
\Omega
of the
\lambda
-calculus. Let
\delta:=\lambda e.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}e,n{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}n
and consider:

\begin{array}[]{lllllll}\Omega&:=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\delta{\shortrightarrow}o{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}o{\varogreaterthan}{\mathsf{!}}\delta,o^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}o^{\prime}&\rightarrow_{\multimap}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}\delta{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}e,n{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}n{\shortrightarrow}o^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}o^{\prime}\\
&&&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}\delta{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}m{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}{\mathsf{!}}\delta,n{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}n{\shortrightarrow}o^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}o^{\prime}\\
&&&\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}\delta{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\delta{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}{\mathsf{!}}\delta,n{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}n{\shortrightarrow}o^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}o^{\prime}\\
&&&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\delta{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}{\mathsf{!}}\delta,n{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}n{\shortrightarrow}o^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}o^{\prime}\\
&&&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\delta{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}{\mathsf{!}}\delta,n{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}n&=_{\alpha}&\Omega\end{array}

Local Termination

A key feature of the untyped ESC (inherited from the LSC) is
local termination
, that is, every rewriting rule is strongly normalizing (SN) when considered separately (not valid in the
\lambda
-calculus which has only one rule). Additionally, the groups of multiplicative rules
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}
and of micro-step exponential rules
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}
are also SN separately, which is instead not valid in untyped classical proof nets, where the exponential rule can diverge as shown by the untyped classical proof net in Fig.
1
(page
1
).

Even the union of
all
the micro-step rules except
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
, noted
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\multimap}}
, is SN. This is due to the fact that
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
is the only rule that
creates
cuts, as explained in Section
6
. Strong normalization of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\multimap}}
is a deep property, pointed out here for the first time. It shows that, for expressivity, rule
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
is as important as duplication, and thus, non-linearity. It also shows that
\multimap
and
\otimes
have completely different computational properties. This is remarkable because in classical linear logic
à la
Girard, the two are collapsed and managed by a single cut elimination rule, the one for
\invamp/\otimes
, and creations are not visible. Together with (non-)termination of untyped exponentials, it is a sign that the intuitionistic setting is sharper than the classical one for studying cut elimination.

Proving Local Termination.

The proofs of local termination and of the termination of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\multimap}}
require various definitions. In particular, we need an auxiliary notion of
variable potential
, which is used to define a
measure
. The measure is then showed to decrease with every micro-step rewriting rule but
\rightarrow_{\multimap}
. A number of auxiliary lemmas are also required.

The intuition behind the potential
\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)
of a variable
x
in
t
is that it is the maximum number of occurrences of
x
that can appear during the cut elimination of
t
, plus the same number for all the variables that are recursively ’nested’ under
x
, where for instance
x
nests under
e
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t
. The key clause is the one for cuts
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t
, which multiplies the potential in
v
for the one of
x
in
t
.

{defi}
[Variable potential]
The potential
\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)
of a variable
x
in
t
is given by:

\begin{array}[]{rll}\mathtt{p}_{x}(y)&:=&0\\
\mathtt{p}_{x}(x)&:=&1\\
\mathtt{p}_{x}((t,s))&:=&\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)+\mathtt{p}_{x}(s)\\
\mathtt{p}_{x}({\mathsf{!}}t)&:=&\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)\\
\mathtt{p}_{x}(\lambda y.t)&:=&\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)\par\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rlll}\mathtt{p}_{x}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}y,z{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t)&:=&\begin{cases}1+\mathtt{p}_{y}(t)+\mathtt{p}_{z}(t)&\mbox{if }x=m\\
\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}\\
\mathtt{p}_{x}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t)&:=&\ \begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }x=m\\
\mathtt{p}_{x}(v)+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}\\
\mathtt{p}_{x}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t)&:=&\begin{cases}1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)+\mathtt{p}_{y}(t)&\mbox{if }x=e\\
\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rll@{\hspace{1.3cm}}rll}\mathtt{p}_{x}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t)&:=&\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)+\mathtt{p}_{x}(v)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{y}(t)+1)\hfil\hskip 36.98866pt\end{array}

The potential is extended to contexts by considering
\langle\cdot\rangle
as a (fresh) exponential variable, that is, having
\mathtt{p}_{x}(\langle\cdot\rangle):=0
,
\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(x):=0
, and
\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(\langle\cdot\rangle):=1
, plus all the expected inductive cases for contexts.

In the definition of the potential, the clause for cut multiplies
\mathtt{p}_{x}(v)
for
\mathtt{p}_{y}(t)+1
where, for exponential variables, the
+1
accounts for the garbage copy of
v
that survives the replacement of all the occurrences of
x
with
v
. Such a
+1
is not needed in the case of multiplicative cuts, but the clause does not distinguish between cuts on multiplicative or exponential variables, treating all variables as if they were exponential. Such an over-estimate is harmless and provides a more compact definition of the potential.

Note also the clause for subtractions: when
x=m
, one could define it as
0
, because anyway cuts on subtractions are ignored, given that we shall prove termination of all rules
but

\rightarrow_{\multimap}
. But it simpler to set it to
1
, as to obtain the useful property of Lemma
21
.
2
below.

{defi}

[Termination measure]

The termination measure
\mathtt{ms}(t)
of a term
t
is defined as follows (and extended to contexts by defining it also for
\langle\cdot\rangle
):

\begin{array}[]{rll\colspace\colspace rll}\mathtt{ms}(x)&:=&1&\mathtt{ms}(\langle\cdot\rangle)&:=&0\\
\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t)&:=&\mathtt{ms}(t)+1&\mathtt{ms}((t,s))&:=&\mathtt{ms}(t)+\mathtt{ms}(s)\\
\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t)&:=&\mathtt{ms}(v)+\mathtt{ms}(t)+1&\mathtt{ms}(\lambda x.t)&:=&\mathtt{ms}(t)\\
\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t)&:=&\mathtt{ms}(t)+1&\mathtt{ms}({\mathsf{!}}t)&:=&\mathtt{ms}(t)\\
\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t)&:=&\mathtt{ms}(v)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(t)\end{array}

The idea behind the measure is that it counts all variable occurrences (that is, variables, pars, derelictions, and subtractions), multiplying for the potential of
x
the occurrences appearing in a value cut on
x
, as for the potential. Each rewriting step removes a variable occurrence, which makes the measure decrease. An exponential rewriting step can also duplicate a sub-term and increase the number of occurrences, but this increment is anticipated by the measure by means of the potential.

For proving that the measure decreases, we need some basic properties of the potential and of the measure plus some lemmas about their decomposition with respect to contexts.

Lemma 21

(Basic properties)
.

(1)

if
x\notin\mathtt{fv}(t)
then
\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)=0
.

(2)

if
x\in\mathtt{fv}(t)
then
\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)\geq 1
.

(3)

\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)\geq 1
.

(4)

\mathtt{ms}(t)\geq 1
.

(5)

\mathtt{ms}(t)=\mathtt{ms}(\{n{\shortrightarrow}m\}t)
.

Proof 8.1
.

Points 1, 2, 4, and 5 are easy inductions on
t
. Point 3 follows from Point 2, since, when looking at the hole
\langle\cdot\rangle
as a variable, one has
\langle\cdot\rangle\in\mathtt{fv}(C)
for every context
C
.

Lemma 22

(Properties for the
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}
cases)
.

(1)

\mathtt{p}_{x}(C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}t\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})=\mathtt{p}_{x}(C)+\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)\cdot\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)
.

(2)

\mathtt{ms}(C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}t\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})=\mathtt{ms}(C)+\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)\cdot\mathtt{ms}(t)
.

Proof 8.2
.

Both points are by induction on
C
, and the second one uses the first one in the cut case. Details are in
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-lics
]
.

Lemma 23

(Properties for the
\rightarrow_{\otimes}
,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}
,
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
cases)
.

(1)

If
C
does not capture variables in
\mathtt{fv}(L)
and
x\notin\mathtt{fv}(L)
then
\mathtt{p}_{x}(C\langle L\langle t\rangle\rangle)=\mathtt{p}_{x}(C\langle t\rangle)
.

(2)

If
C
does not capture
e
and
f
then:

(a)

Potential
:

\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)=\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle t\rangle)+(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)
and
\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)<\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)
.

(b)

Measure
:

\mathtt{ms}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)>\mathtt{ms}(C\langle t\rangle)
and
\mathtt{ms}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)=\mathtt{ms}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)
.

(3)

If
M
does not capture
m
then:

(a)

Potential
:

\mathtt{p}_{m}(M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)=(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t)+\mathtt{p}_{y}(t))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)
.

(b)

Measure
:

\mathtt{ms}(M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)>\mathtt{ms}(M\langle t\rangle)
.

Proof 8.3
.

All points are by induction on the context of the statement. The only non-trivial case, for each point, is the one for cut, which is treated in
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-lics
]
. The others follow immediately from the i.h.

The fourth point of the next lemma shall be used in the proof that the measure decreases for the the
\rightarrow_{\otimes}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
cases. The first three point are intermediate properties to obtain the fourth one.

Lemma 24

(Inequalities for the
\rightarrow_{\otimes}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{!}}}
cases)
.

Let
C
be a context that does not capture free variables in
L
nor
v
, and
L
does not capture variables of
t
.

(1)

\mathtt{p}_{y}(L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)\leq\mathtt{p}_{y}(t)+\mathtt{p}_{y}(L\langle v\rangle)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t))
.

(2)

\mathtt{ms}(L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)\leq\mathtt{ms}(t)+\mathtt{ms}(L\langle v\rangle)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t))
.

(3)

\mathtt{p}_{y}(C\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle)\leq\mathtt{p}_{y}(C\langle t\rangle)+\mathtt{p}_{y}(L\langle v\rangle)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)
.

(4)

\mathtt{ms}(C\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle)\leq\mathtt{ms}(C\langle t\rangle)+\mathtt{ms}(L\langle v\rangle)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)
.

Proof 8.4
.

Point 1 is by induction on
L
, point 2 uses point 1, point 3 and 4 are by induction on
C
using point 1 and 2 in the base case. Details are in
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-lics
]
.

Proposition 25

(Measure decreases)
.

If
t\rightarrow_{a}s
with
a\in\{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2},\otimes,\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2},{\mathsf{!}},\mathtt{w}\}
then
\mathtt{ms}(t)>\mathtt{ms}(s)
.

Proof 8.5
.

By induction on
t\rightarrow_{a}s
. All the inductive cases follow immediately from the i.h. Root cases:

•

\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}
, that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{m}}{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{m}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
. Then:

\begin{array}[]{llllllll}\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{m}}{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})=&\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{m}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{m}(M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})+1)+\mathtt{ms}(M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-is-contextual}.\ref{p:expmeas-is-contextual-one}}}\\
&\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{m}})\cdot(\underbrace{\mathtt{p}_{m}(M)}_{=_{{L.\ref{l:potmul-prop}.\ref{p:potmul-prop-one}}}0}+\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)\cdot\underbrace{\mathtt{p}_{m}(m)}_{=1}+1)+\mathtt{ms}(M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})&=\\
&\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{m}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-is-contextual}.\ref{p:expmeas-is-contextual-two}}}\\
&\underbrace{\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{m}})}_{=_{{L.\ref{l:potmul-prop}.\ref{p:potmul-prop-four}}}1}\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(M)+\underbrace{\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)}_{\geq_{{L.\ref{l:potmul-prop}.\ref{p:potmul-prop-two}}}1}\cdot\mathtt{ms}(m)&>\\
&\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{m}})\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)+\mathtt{ms}(M)&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-is-contextual}.\ref{p:expmeas-is-contextual-two}}}\\
&\mathtt{ms}(M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{m}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})\end{array}

•

\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}
, that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}n{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}u\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}}\{n{\shortrightarrow}m\}u
. Then:

\begin{array}[]{lllllllll}\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}n{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}u)&=&\underbrace{\mathtt{ms}(n)}_{=1}\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{m}(u)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(u)&=\\
&&\mathtt{p}_{m}(u)+1+\mathtt{ms}(u)&>&\mathtt{ms}(u)&=_{{L.\ref{l:potmul-prop}.\ref{p:potmul-prop-five}}}&\mathtt{ms}(\{n{\shortrightarrow}m\}u)\end{array}

•

\mapsto_{\otimes}
, that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}(u,r){\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}p\rangle\mapsto_{\otimes}M\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}L^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}p\rangle\rangle\rangle

with
u=L\langle v\rangle
and
r=L^{\prime}\langle v^{\prime}\rangle
. Then:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}(u,r){\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}p\rangle)&=\\
\mathtt{ms}(M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}p\rangle)+\mathtt{ms}((u,r))\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{m}(M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}p\rangle)+1)&>_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxone}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxone-par-meas}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}(M\langle p\rangle)+\mathtt{ms}((u,r))\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{m}(M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}p\rangle)+1)&=\\
\mathtt{ms}(M\langle p\rangle)+(\mathtt{ms}(u)+\mathtt{ms}(r))\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{m}(M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}p\rangle)+1)&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxone}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxone-par-pm}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}(M\langle p\rangle)+(\underbrace{\mathtt{ms}(u)+\mathtt{ms}(r)}_{\geq_{{L.\ref{l:potmul-prop}.\ref{p:potmul-prop-four}}}1})\cdot((1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(p)+\mathtt{p}_{y}(p))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)+1)&>\\
\mathtt{ms}(M\langle p\rangle)+(\mathtt{ms}(u)+\mathtt{ms}(r))\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(p)+\mathtt{p}_{y}(p))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)&=\\
\mathtt{ms}(M\langle p\rangle)+\mathtt{ms}(u)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(p)+\mathtt{p}_{y}(p))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)+\mathtt{ms}(r)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(p)+\mathtt{p}_{y}(p))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)&\geq\\
\mathtt{ms}(M\langle p\rangle)+\mathtt{ms}(u)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{y}(p))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)+\mathtt{ms}(r)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(p))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)&=\\
\underbrace{\mathtt{ms}(M\langle p\rangle)+\mathtt{ms}(L^{\prime}\langle v^{\prime}\rangle)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{y}(p))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)}_{=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxtwo}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxtwo-four}}}\mathtt{ms}(M\langle L^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}p\rangle\rangle)}+\mathtt{ms}(L\langle v\rangle)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(p))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)&=\\
\mathtt{ms}(M\langle L^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}p\rangle\rangle)+\mathtt{ms}(L\langle v\rangle)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(p))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxone}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxone-two}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}(M\langle L^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}p\rangle\rangle)+\mathtt{ms}(L\langle v\rangle)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(L^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}p\rangle))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(M)&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxtwo}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxtwo-four}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}(M\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}L^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v^{\prime}{\shortrightarrow}y{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}p\rangle\rangle\rangle)\end{array}

•

\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}
, that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{e}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
.

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})&=\\
\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-is-contextual}.\ref{p:expmeas-is-contextual-one}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C)+\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)\cdot\underbrace{\mathtt{p}_{e}(e)}_{=1}+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})&=\\
\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C)+\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})&=\\
\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)+\mathtt{ms}(C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-is-contextual}.\ref{p:expmeas-is-contextual-two}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)+\mathtt{ms}(C)+\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)\cdot\underbrace{\mathtt{ms}(e)}_{=1}&=\\
\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C)+1)+\underbrace{\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)+\mathtt{ms}(C)}_{=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-is-contextual}.\ref{p:expmeas-is-contextual-two}}}\mathtt{ms}(C\langle v_{\mathtt{e}}\rangle)}+\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)&=\\
\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle v_{\mathtt{e}}\rangle)+\underbrace{\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)}_{>_{{L.\ref{l:potmul-prop}.\ref{p:potmul-prop-two}}}0}&>\\
\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle v_{\mathtt{e}}\rangle)&=_{{L.\ref{l:potmul-prop}.\ref{p:potmul-prop-one}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C)+\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)\cdot\underbrace{\mathtt{p}_{e}(v_{\mathtt{e}})}_{=_{{L.\ref{l:potmul-prop}.\ref{p:potmul-prop-one}}}0}+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{e}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-is-contextual}.\ref{p:expmeas-is-contextual-one}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{e}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{e}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})&=\\
\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{e}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})\end{array}

•

\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}
, that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{e}_{2}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle
. Then:

\begin{array}[]{lllllll}\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)&=&\mathtt{ms}(f)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)&>_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxone}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxone-der-pm}}}\\
&&\mathtt{ms}(f)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxone}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxone-der-meas}}}\\
&&\mathtt{ms}(f)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)&=\\
&&\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}f{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}f{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)\end{array}

•

\mapsto_{{\mathsf{!}}}
, that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}s{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\mapsto_{{\mathsf{!}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}s{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle
with
s=L\langle v\rangle
. Then:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}s{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)&=\\
\mathtt{ms}({\mathsf{!}}s)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)&>_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxone}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxone-der-meas}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}({\mathsf{!}}s)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle t\rangle)&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxone}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxone-der-pm}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}({\mathsf{!}}s)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle t\rangle)+(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle t\rangle)&=\\
\mathtt{ms}({\mathsf{!}}s)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle t\rangle)+1)+\mathtt{ms}({\mathsf{!}}s)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle t\rangle)&=\\
\mathtt{ms}({\mathsf{!}}s)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle t\rangle)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(s)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle t\rangle)&=\\
\mathtt{ms}({\mathsf{!}}s)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle t\rangle)+1)+\underbrace{\mathtt{ms}(L\langle v\rangle)\cdot(1+\mathtt{p}_{x}(t))\cdot\mathtt{p}_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(C)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle t\rangle)}_{=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxtwo}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxtwo-four}}}\,\mathtt{ms}(C\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle)}\par&=\\
\mathtt{ms}({\mathsf{!}}s)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle t\rangle)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle)&=_{{L.\ref{l:expmeas-auxone}.\ref{p:expmeas-auxone-two}}}\\
\mathtt{ms}({\mathsf{!}}s)\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(C\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(C\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle)&=\\
\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}{\mathsf{!}}s{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C\langle L\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle)\end{array}

•

\mapsto_{\mathtt{w}}
, that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t\mapsto_{\mathtt{w}}t
with
e\notin\mathtt{fv}(t)
. Then:

\mathtt{ms}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{e}}{\shortrightarrow}e{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}t)\ =\ \mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})\cdot(\mathtt{p}_{e}(t)+1)+\mathtt{ms}(t)\ =_{{L.\ref{l:potmul-prop}.\ref{p:potmul-prop-one}}}\ \mathtt{ms}(v_{\mathtt{e}})+\mathtt{ms}(t)\ >_{{L.\ref{l:potmul-prop}.\ref{p:potmul-prop-four}}}\ \mathtt{ms}(t).\hfill

Theorem 26

(Local termination)
.

Let
a\in\{\mathtt{m},\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}},\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}},\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\multimap},\mathtt{ss}_{\neg\multimap}\}
. Then
\rightarrow_{a}
is strongly normalizing.

Proof 8.6
.

For
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}_{\neg\multimap}}
it is an immediate consequence of the fact that the measure decreases (Prop.
25
) and that it is always positive (Lemma
21
.
4
). The result is extended to
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ss}_{\neg\multimap}}
by full composition (Prop.
11
). For
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}
, the statement is obvious, as at each step
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}
decreases the number of constructors.

The literature contains other partitions of linear logic cut elimination in strongly normalizing reductions: Danos
[
Danos:Thesis:90
]
and Joinet
[
joinetthesis
]
split it into axiom and non-axiom rewriting rules, Pagani and Tortora de Falco
[
DBLP:journals/tcs/PaganiF10
]
into
structural
(a subset of exponential) and
logical
(i.e. non-structural). We are interested in the lolli/non-lolli partition because it mimics what happens in
\lambda
-calculi with ESs, and also because of the future work described in
the next step
paragraph of the conclusions, for which the mentioned alternative partitions in the literature would not work.

Next

Three independent topics follow: confluence (Section
10
), the good strategy (Section
), and strong normalization (Sections
and
). They can be read in any order. First, however, we need to develop some technical tools for dealing with contexts in proofs.

9.
Some Technicalities

This section develops definitions and technical tools that are used in the proofs of the following sections to manage contexts, namely the outside-in order on contexts,
contexts with
two
holes (called
double contexts
), and a technical but important
deformation lemma
. At a first (or even second) reading, the reader can skip this section.

{defi}

[Outside-in context order, disjoint contexts]
We define the partial
outside-in
order
\prec
over contexts as follows:

\langle\cdot\rangle\prec C

C\prec C^{\prime}

C^{\prime\prime}\langle C\rangle\prec C^{\prime\prime}\langle C^{\prime}\rangle

And say that
C
is
outer
than
C^{\prime}
if
C\prec C^{\prime}
. If
C\not\prec C^{\prime}
and

C^{\prime}\not\prec C
we say that
C
and
C^{\prime}
are
disjoint
, and write
C\parallel C^{\prime}
.

Double Contexts.

Double contexts shall be used to compare two contexts on the same term. They have as base
cases binary constructors (that is, tensor pairs, cuts, and subtractions) having contexts replacing their subterms, and
as inductive cases they are simply closed by an ordinary context.

{defi}
[Double contexts]
Double contexts
\mathbb{C}
are defined by the following grammar.

\begin{array}[]{l\colspace\colspace llll}\textsc{Double contexts}&\mathbb{C}&:=&(C,C^{\prime})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}C,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}C^{\prime}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}C{\shortrightarrow}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}C^{\prime}\mid C\langle\mathbb{C}\rangle\end{array}

Some easy facts about double contexts.

•

Plugging
: the plugging
\mathbb{C}\langle t,s\rangle
of two terms
t
and
s
into a double context
\mathbb{C}

is defined as expected and gives a term. The two ways of plugging one term
\mathbb{C}\langle t,\langle\cdot\rangle\rangle
and

\mathbb{C}\langle\langle\cdot\rangle,s\rangle
into a double context give instead a context. We often use
\mathbb{C}\langle t,\cdot\rangle
and

\mathbb{C}\langle\cdot,s\rangle
as compact notations for
\mathbb{C}\langle t,\langle\cdot\rangle\rangle
and
\mathbb{C}\langle\langle\cdot\rangle,s\rangle
.

•

Pairs of disjoint positions and double contexts
: every pair of positions
C\langle t\rangle=C^{\prime}\langle s\rangle
which
are disjoint, that is, such that
C\parallel C^{\prime}
, gives rise to a double context
\mathbb{C}_{C,C^{\prime}}
such
that
\mathbb{C}_{C,C^{\prime}}\langle\cdot,s\rangle=C
and
\mathbb{C}_{C,C^{\prime}}\langle t,\cdot\rangle=C^{\prime}
.
Conversely, every double context
\mathbb{C}
and every pair of terms
t
and
s
gives rise to two context

\mathbb{C}_{l}^{t,s}:=\mathbb{C}\langle\cdot,s\rangle
and
C_{r}^{t,s}:=\mathbb{C}\langle t,\cdot\rangle
. Note that
the two constructions are inverse of each other, that is, given
\mathbb{C}
, we have

\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{C}_{l}^{t,s},C_{r}^{t,s}}=\mathbb{C}
, and, conversely, given
C\langle t\rangle=C^{\prime}\langle s\rangle
we
have
(\mathbb{C}_{C,C^{\prime}})_{l}^{t,s}=C
and
(\mathbb{C}_{C,C^{\prime}})_{r}^{t,s}=C^{\prime}
.

Lemma 27
.

C\langle\mathbb{C}\langle t,\cdot\rangle\rangle=C\langle\mathbb{C}\rangle\langle t,\cdot\rangle
and
C\langle\mathbb{C}\langle\cdot,t\rangle\rangle=C\langle\mathbb{C}\rangle\langle\cdot,t\rangle
.

Proof 9.1
.

By induction on
C
.

Deformation Lemma

We need a set of
deformation lemmas
, collected together in
Lemma
28
, that say what happens to a variable occurrence under the action
of a rewriting step: for instance, given a step
M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}s
, one lemma states that
s
has shape
M^{\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
unless the step reduces a cut containing
m
. The idea is that then
M^{\prime}
is the residual of
M
, but the statement(s) avoid using any form of residuals of steps, sub-terms, or constructors. The residual aspect is captured by specifying the preservation modulo a parameter—in
the case mentioned above, this is obtained stating that there is a step
M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{m}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\rightarrow_{a}M^{\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{m}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
for every
v_{\mathtt{m}}
.

The deformation lemmas shall be used in the following sections, namely in the proofs of local confluence (where preservation modulo a parameter is exploited), for the diamond property of the good strategy (for which the deformation lemmas shall have to be strengthened), and for the root cut expansion property (Prop.
) at work in both the preservation of untyped strong normalization and typed normalization.

Lemma 28

(Deformation properties)
.

Let
\rightarrow\in\{\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ss}},\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}\}
.

(1)

Let
C:t=M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\rightarrow_{a}s
and
\rightarrow_{a}
be a
\rightarrow
step. Then:

(a)

either
s=M^{\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
for some

M^{\prime}
such that
m\notin\mathtt{fv}(M^{\prime})
and there is a step
C^{\prime}_{v_{\mathtt{m}}}:M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{m}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\rightarrow_{a}M^{\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{m}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
for every
v_{\mathtt{m}}
,

(b)

or
M=M^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}\langle\cdot\rangle{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}u\rangle
for some

M^{\prime}
and
C
reduces the cut on
n
.

(2)

Let
C:t=M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}t_{m}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\rightarrow_{a}s
where
t_{m}
is a par or a subtraction of conclusion
m
,

M^{\prime}
does not capture
n
, and
\rightarrow_{a}
be a
\rightarrow
step. Then
s=M^{\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}s_{m}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
where

s_{m}
is an occurrence of
m
of the same kind of
t_{m}
,

M^{\prime}
does not capture
m
and
m\notin\mathtt{fv}(M^{\prime})
, and there is a step
C^{\prime}_{n}:M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\{n{\shortrightarrow}m\}t_{m}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\rightarrow_{a}M^{\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\{n{\shortrightarrow}m\}s_{m}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
for every fresh
multiplicative variable
n
.

(3)

If
C:M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rightarrow_{a}s
is a

\rightarrow
-step and
M
does not
capture
m
then

s=M^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t^{\prime}\rangle
with
M^{\prime}
not capturing
m
and
such that there is a step
C^{\prime}_{C^{\prime\prime}}:M\langle C^{\prime\prime}\langle t\rangle\rangle\rightarrow_{a}M^{\prime}\langle C^{\prime\prime}\langle t^{\prime}\rangle\rangle
for every
context
C^{\prime\prime}
capturing no more than
x
and
y
in
t
and such that
M
does not capture
variables of
C^{\prime\prime}
.

(4)

If
C:M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}r\rangle\rightarrow_{a}s
is a

\rightarrow
-step and
M
does not
capture
m
then either

•

s=M^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v,x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}r^{\prime}\rangle
with

C^{\prime}_{C^{\prime\prime}}:M\langle C^{\prime\prime}\langle r\rangle\rangle\rightarrow_{a}M^{\prime}\langle C^{\prime\prime}\langle r^{\prime}\rangle\rangle
for every context
C^{\prime\prime}
capturing no more than
x
in
r
and such
that

M
does not capture in
C^{\prime\prime}
more variables than those in
\mathtt{fv}(v)
, or

•

s=M^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\varogreaterthan}v^{\prime},x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}r\rangle
with
C^{\prime}_{C^{\prime\prime}}:M\langle C^{\prime\prime}\langle v\rangle\rangle\rightarrow_{a}M^{\prime}\langle C^{\prime\prime}\langle v^{\prime}\rangle\rangle
for every context
C^{\prime\prime}
not capturing variables of
v
and such that
M

does not capture in
C^{\prime\prime}
more variables than those in
\mathtt{fv}(r)\setminus\{x\}
.

In both cases
M^{\prime}
does not capture
m
.

(5)

Let
C:t=C^{\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\rightarrow_{a}s
and
\rightarrow_{a}
be a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}
step. Then:

(a)

either
s=C^{\prime\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
for some

C^{\prime\prime}
such that there is a step
C^{\bullet}_{v_{\mathtt{e}}}:C^{\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{e}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\rightarrow_{a}C^{\prime\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{e}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
for
every

v_{\mathtt{e}}
,

(b)

or
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}C^{\prime\prime\prime}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}u\rangle
for some

C^{\prime\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and
C
reduces a redex where the active cut is the one on
f
.

(6)

Let
C:t=C^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u\rangle\rightarrow_{a}s
with
C^{\prime}
not capturing
e
and
\rightarrow_{a}

be
a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}
step. Then:

(a)

either
s=C^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}e{{\mathsf{?}}}x{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}u^{\prime}\rangle
for some

C^{\prime\prime}
such that there is a step
C^{\bullet}_{C^{\prime\prime}}:C^{\prime}\langle C^{\prime\prime}\langle u\rangle\rangle\rightarrow_{a}C^{\prime\prime}\langle C^{\prime\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle\rangle
for every
C^{\prime\prime}
capturing no more than
x
in

and

u^{\prime}
,

(b)

or
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}C^{\prime\prime\prime}{\shortrightarrow}f{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}u\rangle
for some
C^{\prime\prime}
and

C^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and
C
reduces a redex given by the cut on
f
.

Proof 9.2
.

The proof is by induction on the rewriting step in each point, and is a tedious check. In
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-lics
]
, we give all the (many!) details for the first deformation property. The proofs of the others are minor variations.

10.
Untyped Confluence

Here we prove confluence for the untyped ESC, using an elegant technique based on local diagrams and local termination. The technique is the Hindley-Rosen method. In our case, it amounts to prove that the multiplicative and exponential rules
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}
are confluent separately, proved by local termination and Newman lemma, and commute, proved by local termination and Hindley’s strong commutation. Confluence then follows by Hindley-Rosen lemma, for which the union of confluent and commuting reductions is confluent.

The Hindley-Rosen method is a modular technique often used for confluence of extensions of the
\lambda
-calculus, for instance in
[
ArrighiD17
,
DBLP:conf/lics/FaggianR19
,
DBLP:conf/csl/Saurin08
,
DBLP:conf/fossacs/CarraroG14
,
Revesz92
,
DBLP:journals/lmcs/BucciarelliKR21
,
AriolaFMOW95
,
DBLP:conf/flops/AccattoliP12
]
. It is also used for untyped proof nets by Pagani and Tortora de Falco
[
DBLP:journals/tcs/PaganiF10
]
. Confluence for untyped proof nets is also proved by Danos
[
Danos:Thesis:90
]
and Tranquilli
[
DBLP:conf/csl/Tranquilli09
]
via finite developments. Essentially, local termination internalizes finite developments.

The Glitch

For the untyped ESC there is a slight flaw due to clashes. The following local diagram, indeed, can be closed only if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}
is not a clashing cut (precisely, when
v
is not an exponential value nor an abstraction), or with cut equivalence
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
:
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}m{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}n{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle
M^{\prime}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}n{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}n{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M^{\prime}\langle M\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}m{\invamp}x,y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}t\rangle\rangle
\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}
\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2}

A second similarly problematic diagram is obtained by replacing the par with a subtraction.

Now, if there is a clash, the fact that confluence holds only up
to
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
is irrelevant: the clash is a bigger issue. Moreover:

(1)

(Recursive) types remove clashes:
clashes are ruled out by our typing (Lemma
15
), but also by recursive types such as those used for typing the untyped
\lambda
-calculus, or even the more general weak recursive typing considered by Ehrhard and Regnier in
[
DBLP:journals/tcs/EhrhardR06
]
.

(2)

Proof nets are not better
: because of weakenings, IMELL proof nets need
jumps
and jump
rewiring rules
. Confluence should then be proved
up to jump rewiring
, which is analogous to confluence up to
\equiv_{\mathtt{cut}}
.

(3)

Good steps are glitch-free
: anticipating from the next section, the glitch does not affect
good steps
, as the
\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}
steps in the diagram above is
bad
.

The Proof

We first prove the local properties.

Proposition 29

(Local confluence)
.

(1)

Multiplicative clash-free diamond
:

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}
is diamond on clash-free terms.

(2)

Exponential local confluence
:
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}
are both
locally confluent.

Proof 10.1
.

(1)

The formal statement is:
t_{1}\;\mbox{}_{\mathtt{m}}{\leftarrow}t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}t_{2}
with
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
implies that there exists
t_{3}
such that
t_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}t_{3}\;\mbox{}_{\mathtt{m}}{\leftarrow}t_{2}
. Let
a,b\in\{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{2},\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1},\otimes,\multimap\}
,
t_{0}\rightarrow_{a}t_{1}
, and
t_{0}\rightarrow_{b}t_{2}
. The proof is by induction on
t_{0}\rightarrow_{a}t_{1}
, that is, by induction on the context
C
closing the root rule
\mapsto_{a}
, and case analysis of
t_{0}\rightarrow_{b}t_{2}
. Here we show only the case for
\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}
, which is the most interesting one, requiring the deformation lemma and allowing us to discuss the case of clashes. The other cases are in
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-lics
]
.

We have
t_{0}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{[}}v_{\mathtt{m}}{\shortrightarrow}m{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{]}}M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\mapsto_{\mathtt{ax}\mathtt{m}_{1}}M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{\mathtt{m}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=t_{1}
.
If
\rightarrow_{b}
takes place entirely in
v_{\mathtt{m}}
then the diagram closes in one step on both
sides.
If
\rightarrow_{b}
takes place in
M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
, that is,
M\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\rightarrow_{b}s
then by deformation (Lemma
28
.
1
) there are two mutually exclusive cases:

(ii)

C
and
C^{\prime}
are disjoint
. Then, the reasoning goes similarly to the case where
t_{e}
is a variable occurrence, resting on a double context, and it has two straightforward sub-cases depending on
v_{\mathtt{e}}
, as when

.

In contrast to confluence, commutation of two reductions
\rightarrow_{1}
and
\rightarrow_{2}
does
not
follow from their
local
commutation and strong normalization. Here however the rules verify a
linear
form of Hindley’s
strong
(local) commutation
[
HindleyPhD
]
of
\rightarrow_{1}
over
\rightarrow_{2}
, defined as:

t
s_{1}
s_{2}
1
2

implies that there exists
u
such that

t
s_{1}
s_{2}
u
1
2
*
2
1

That is,
\rightarrow_{2}
cannot duplicate nor erase
\rightarrow_{1}
. Linear commutation and strong normalization do imply commutation.

Proposition 30

(Linear commutation)
.

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ss}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}
both linearly commute over
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}
.

Proof 10.2
.

The proof is an analysis of cases very similar to the one for local confluence. Some details are in
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-lics
]
.

Then we lift the local properties using local termination and conclude using Hindley-Rosen lemma.

Theorem 31

(Confluence)
.

The relations
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ss}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}
are confluent on clash-free terms.

Proof 10.3
.

We treat
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}
, the case of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ss}}
is identical. By Newman lemma, local confluence (Prop.
29
), and local termination (Theorem
26
),
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}
are confluent separately. By a result of Hindley
[
HindleyPhD
]
, linear commutation (Prop.
30
) and local termination imply that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}
commute. By Hindley-Rosen lemma,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ms}}=\rightarrow_{\mathtt{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}^{\mathtt{ms}}}
is confluent.
